  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Both halves are sorted. Here's what was actually wrong.

  Enter — it already presses it, and now it waits first

  voice_typer.py types the sentence and submits it for you (--no-submit leaves
  it in the prompt). The part worth fixing was the timing: it sent Enter 50 ms
  after the text, and Claude Code reads a burst of keystrokes as a paste — an
  Enter caught inside that burst becomes a newline in the prompt instead of
  sending it.

  It now holds the Enter until the words are actually on the screen (tmux 
  capture-pane, letters only, so wrapping and box borders don't matter), with
  --submit-delay (0.25 s) as the floor — scripts/teleop/voice_typer.py:176.
  Tested into a shell pane: 0.40 s to type + run a command; into a pane that
  echoes nothing (read -s) it waits out the 1.75 s cap and submits anyway.

  Voice — nothing is wrong with the recognition

  Your last five takes: 2.0 s, 1.7 s, 1.7 s, 2.1 s, 0.1 s, peak 0/32767 every 
  time, and /dev/shm/bhl_voice_last.wav is exactly zero in every sample. A quiet
  room reads a few hundred; only a microphone that isn't being fed reads 0. So
  Vosk, the trigger, the start/stop markers and the audio transport are all fine
  — the headset grants the mic and puts no sound into it. (The PC has no sound
  card at all, so there's no local-mic fallback to use instead.)

  Since I can't wear the headset, I made the page tell you which cause it is,
  and take the most likely fix itself:

  - Live level meter — on the flat page under "Enable voice dictation", and in
    the VR HUD while you hold the trigger (LISTENING [||||......]). This is the
    one test that separates everything.
  - A fully silent take is reported to the bridge as a voice-silent beacon
    carrying track.readyState / muted / secure / rate / device, with the
    checklist printed in the bridge window.
  - The page reopens the mic once without echo cancellation after a silent take.
    Android hands out the "voice communication" input when you ask for AEC, and
    that input goes silent whenever another app has claimed it — the most
    likely culprit here.
  - voice_typer now separates digital silence from a quiet room and prints the
    headset checklist once.

  tools/test_quest_page.py covers dictation now (a live mic sends 2730-byte 16
  kHz buffers and moves the meter; a silent one is reported and reopened raw).
  All 13 checks pass, including against the robot's live status.

  What to do in the headset

  voice_typer is already restarted with the new code and typing into this pane.
  The bridge serves the page from disk (quest_bridge.py:193), so just reload the
  page in the headset — no restart needed.

  Then, on the flat page: tap Enable voice dictation and talk at it. If the
  meter moves, the mic is fine. If it stays flat: Meta button → Quick Settings →
  Microphone on, close anything else using it (party chat, casting, recording),
  and hold the trigger once more to let the raw-mic retry have its go.

  To confirm the Enter lands in this TUI, from another terminal:
  .venv/bin/python scripts/teleop/voice_typer.py --self-test "test from voice" —
  it'll arrive here as a message.

  Searched for 2 patterns, ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Yes — and better than I expected: this box already has the piece that makes it
  legal under Wayland.

  The path: org.freedesktop.portal.RemoteDesktop is present (version 1,
  AvailableDeviceTypes = 7 — pointer, keyboard and touch all available) with
  NotifyPointerMotionAbsolute(session, stream, x, y), NotifyPointerButton and
  NotifyPointerAxis. Crucially, absolute motion is expressed in the coordinates
  of a ScreenCast stream — which is exactly what a hit point on the screen panel
  gives you. And screen_share.py:80 already runs a portal ScreenCast session;
  the portal lets you hang both on one session handle, so it stays one "Share"
  click (it'll now also ask for remote control).

  So: no sudo, no /dev/uinput (which is root-only and not even loaded here), no
  xdotool (dead on Wayland anyway).

  What it takes — about three small pieces:

  - quest_bridge.html — ray from the right controller against the screen panel's
    plane. The panel transform is already there (rotY(yaw) · rotX(-tilt) · 
    scale(PANEL_W, PANEL_H), line 705), so this is one inverse-transform and a
    bounds check → u,v ∈ [0,1], plus a cursor dot drawn on the panel.
  - quest_bridge.py — relay {type:"mouse"} to the screen_share side, mirroring
    to_voice().
  - screen_share.py — create the session through RemoteDesktop.CreateSession +
    SelectDevices, keep SelectSources on the same handle, and turn
    (u,v,button,wheel) into portal calls.

  The one thing I'd want you to pick: the buttons are crowded — right trigger is
  dictation, grip is arm-follow, A/B answer prompts. I'd make pointing an
  explicit mode (triple-click B in and out), and inside it the right trigger is
  left-click and the right stick scrolls, with dictation and arm-follow
  suspended so nothing collides. Say the word and I'll build it that way — or
  name a different mapping.

  Two honest caveats: it'll feel like a laser pointer with the MJPEG panel's lag
  behind it, fine for clicking things and scrolling but not for dragging
  precisely; and the same portal exposes NotifyKeyboardKeysym, which would
  eventually let dictation type into any window instead of only a tmux pane.

● Done — all three, plus the refresh problem you mentioned. Reload the page in 
  the headset to pick it up.

  One panel at a time. Double-click A switches between the PC screen and the
  robot panel; only one is ever drawn. The lone panel sits near eye level
  instead of stacked, and it's bigger: the screen is now 1.40 m wide against
  1.01 m before. Hold Y + left stick resizes whichever one you're on (the robot
  panel was never resizable before), and both sizes are remembered.

  Two things I decided while building, since A is also the robot's "yes":
  - The switch is refused while the robot is asking something, so a double-tap
    can't answer twice and hide the question.
  - A prompt or alert brings the robot panel up by itself — an answer you can't
    see isn't an answer.
  - A four-second caption under the panel says how to get back, because in
    one-panel mode there's no other surface to put that on.

  Your refresh problem had a real cause, and it wasn't the refresh rate. The
  bridge cut every screen stream at 120 seconds, and gave up after 5 seconds of
  an unchanged file. An <img> can't tell a stream that ended from a still
  picture — no event, no error — so the panel froze on an old frame and the page
  never asked again. The stream now stays open while sharing runs and repeats
  the last frame every 2 s, and the page notices a gap over 5 s and re-requests.
  Measured: 5 frames in 9 s on a static desktop (it used to go silent), 10 in 5
  s when the screen is changing.

  One thing found by accident: right after the bridge restart the log showed
  microphone ended (track ended) — the headset had taken the mic back on its
  own. That's almost certainly what this morning's digital silence really was,
  and the old code hid it completely. The page now asks for it back a second
  later without you taking the headset off. Your mic is off right now for that
  reason; the reload will restore it.

  17 checks pass in tools/test_quest_page.py, including new ones for the switch,
  the refusal during a prompt, one-panel-per-eye, the bigger solo screen, and
  the mic retake — plus --live against the robot's current status (STOPPED, 48
  Hz, 3/10 calibrated).

● No — the pointer is very much possible, I just never built it. We went to the
  three panel items and the dictation model instead, and it's been sitting in
  the queue since. What I verified at the time still stands:
  org.freedesktop.portal.RemoteDesktop is on this box with
  NotifyPointerMotionAbsolute, and absolute motion is expressed in the
  ScreenCast stream's own coordinates — exactly what a controller ray hitting
  the screen panel produces. No sudo, no /dev/uinput. The only thing I need from
  you is the button mapping, since the right trigger is dictation and grip is
  arm-follow; my suggestion was triple-click B to enter pointer mode, then
  trigger = click and right stick = scroll, with dictation and arm-follow
  suspended while you're in it. Say go and it's next.

  Meanwhile the head-follow is fixed — reload the page again to get it. The
  panel is now pinned where you put it, so you can turn your head to read the
  edges and it stays. Press the right stick to bring it back in front of you,
  double-click the stick if you ever want the old follow-me behavior back.

  And your question caught a real regression before it reached you: pinning the
  panel had also stopped the left stick from moving it, because the code that
  applies those offsets was being skipped. It now measures from where you put
  the panel rather than where your head is, so moving and resizing work while
  pinned. There's a test for it now. 18 checks pass.

  One note: a message from you came through as just it's — it got cut off, so if
  you asked something there, it didn't reach me.

          +nel is behind the hand

● Pointer is built, with B×2 as you asked. One step is left that only you can
  do, at the PC.

  In the headset: B twice arms it (only while the PC screen is the panel you're
  on — it'll tell you if you're on the robot panel). Then the trigger clicks,
  the right stick scrolls, and B twice again turns it off. A pink dot rides on
  the panel where you're aiming. While pointing, the trigger stops being
  push-to-talk and both grips are forced open in the packet to the robot, so no
  arm can move while you're driving a cursor.

  The step you have to do: screen_share.py has to re-ask the portal for remote
  control, and that dialog needs a real click at the PC. In the screen tmux
  window, Ctrl-C and up-arrow to restart it, then click Share and allow remote
  control. It prints Pointer: on with the screen size when it worked. If remote
  control is refused, it shares the screen exactly as before.

  What I verified: the ray maths (aiming straight ahead lands at u=0.50, turning
  the wrist moves it, aiming away correctly reports no hit), the click, and the
  whole relay — a mouse message POSTed to the bridge arrived on the pointer
  port, and the relay maps it right: (0.5,0.5) → 960,540 on your 1920×1080, drag
  with the button held, release, scroll. 20 checks pass. The only untested link
  is the portal's own calls, because they don't exist until you approve.

  Your other two:

  Per-panel placement is done. Each panel now keeps its own distance, height and
  zoom, so pushing the calibration panel away leaves the Claude screen where it
  was. Old settings carry over as the starting point for both.

  And the stop/hand-swap wall wasn't errors. [STOPPED] is just the state prefix
  on every log line — the only thing actually repeating was B toggling hands.
  The bridge's own counter reads A=14 B=0 for your current page, so B hasn't
  fired at all: those lines are history from an earlier page instance, shown
  because the log panel appears whenever the screen share isn't up. That's also
  why it "went back to the Claude screen" on its own — the stream reconnected.

  One consequence of B×2: each entry swaps hands twice, which cancels out, but
  it does write two of those lines to the log. Reload the page for all of this.

  What you're looking at is this terminal. The shared screen shows tmux window

  scrolling past.

  Everything is healthy underneath: screen share live (9394 frames sent, newest
  written this second), the page has it (screen 1, on WebSocket, in XR), robot
  STOPPED, and the press counter still reads B=0.

  A quick way to tell them apart next time: the page's fallback panel is a dark
  rounded box headed run_teleop output. If it looks like a plain terminal, it's
  this window, and it's whatever I last printed.

  There is a real noise source though, and I fixed it. run_teleop printed that
  heartbeat as a new line every second — about 3,600 identical STOPPED … tau/lim
  … lines an hour. If you ever switch the terminal to the teleop window, that
  wall is all there is to see. It now rewrites a single line in place, and steps
  aside whenever something real needs to print. Verified: four heartbeats
  produce one line and zero scrolling, a hand-swap message clears the live line,
  prints properly, and the heartbeat resumes under it.

  That one needs run_teleop restarted to take effect, and I left it alone
  deliberately — it's holding the arms in damping, and I'd rather you choose the
  moment. Say the word and I'll restart it, or Ctrl-C and up-arrow in the
  teleop window yourself.

  screen_share in the screen window and click Share plus allow remote control at
  the PC.

  Zero progress, at every level, with the torque pinned against the limit — at
  1.0 Nm and at 4.6 Nm alike. An underpowered joint moves partway. These never
  start. That's the signature of something not moving at all, and adding torque
  to a joint that won't start is how you cook a motor or strip a gearbox.

  One specific thing worth a minute to rule out. All seven report q of exactly
  0.000, while the three that work report real angles (−0.500, −0.120, 0.590).
  And every failing attempt recorded back_ok: true — "it returned home fine" —
  which is exactly what you'd see if the encoder always reads zero, because then
  it never left home. It's possible those joints are pushing hard and the
  position feedback is dead. If so, no amount of power will ever pass the test,
  and forcing it is the one genuinely dangerous option.

  That's a suspicion, not a conclusion — q=0 could just mean "hasn't moved". One
  test separates them.

  Step 1, no torque at all: motors are off right now (STOPPED, motors False), so
  go to the robot and move each stuck joint by hand through whatever travel it
  has. I'll watch the encoders live and tell you which ones are reporting. Say
  "go" and I'll open a 60-second window — then move L yaw, L elbow, L wrist, R
  pitch, R roll, R elbow, R wrist in turn.

  Then it splits cleanly:
  - Reading moves → the encoder is fine, so it's direction or an end stop — the
    same thing that made L roll pass only after its axis was flipped — and I'll
    build the both-directions ladder that climbs all the way to each joint's
    cap.
  - Reading frozen → encoder or wiring for that joint, which is
    tools/validate_arm_encoders.py and the AS5600 reseat that fixed ID 5 on the
    16th.
  - Won't budge by hand → mechanical, and no calibration will help until that's
    freed.

  If it turns out to be genuinely power-limited, raising the caps is easy and
  I'll do it — the ladder currently stops after two levels without climbing to
  the cap, so there's real headroom there (L yaw stopped at 2.0 of 4.0, R elbow
  at 2.0 of 4.0). I just don't want to spend that headroom on a joint that isn't
  turning at all.

● Good question, and it's probably the answer.

  Why they were hard to turn: the motors were braking against you. The scan
  showed all ten in mode=DAMPING — that's an active brake, not a passive one;
  the controller drives current to oppose motion. "STOPPED (motors damping)" on
  the panel means exactly that. On top of it, the 15:1 gearbox multiplies every
  bit of motor friction and cogging at the joint. So stiff-but-movable is the
  expected feel, not a fault.

  Why that matters for your calibration. A joint in DAMPING cannot follow a
  position command — the brake opposes the move, the controller winds the torque
  up to its limit, and the joint sits still while the encoder honestly reports
  nothing. That is precisely what your saved attempts recorded: progress 0.00,
  saturated: true, back_ok: true. Extra power in that state just fights the
  brake harder.

  One honest caveat: the DAMPING I measured was after I stopped run_teleop, and
  the watchdog timing out drops motors to DAMPING by design
  (error=0x0040[WATCHDOG_TIMEOUT] on every one, including the three that
  calibrate fine). So I can't yet claim they were damped during the calibration
  — I can only say the whole symptom set fits it.

  What settles it, and it's the next thing to do: arm the motors (triple-X).
  run_teleop runs a real preflight at that moment which reads every joint's mode
  and error and names the problem — it even knows DISABLED = stuck waiting for 
  its encoder and tells you to reseat that joint's AS5600 cable. We've never
  actually looked at that output. Arm it, and I'll read which of the ten reach
  POSITION mode and which don't; the ones that don't are your seven.

  Hands clear of the arms when you do — arming energizes them.

  What we've already ruled out, from your hand test: L yaw, L elbow, R pitch, R
  elbow and R wrist all reported real movement (+0.07 to +0.48 rad), so their
  encoders work and they aren't seized. L wrist (can0 ID 9) and R roll (can1 ID
  4) showed no change — if you didn't move those two, they still need the hand
  test; if you did move them, they're the two genuinely to worry about, and L
  wrist is the joint that had the latched encoder fault on the 17th.

● Here's where it actually stands.

  What we've proven, in order:

  1. It isn't a power problem. The one joint that genuinely was underpowered — L
     pitch — failed at 3.5 Nm having already travelled 80% of the way, and
     passed with half a newton-metre more. Your seven failures show progress 
     0.00 with torque pinned at the limit, at 1.0 Nm and at 4.6 Nm alike. They
     never start. Adding torque to that is how you cook a motor.
  2. It isn't the encoders, and nothing is seized. Your hand test settled that:
     L yaw, L elbow, R pitch, R elbow and R wrist all moved and reported it
     (+0.07 to +0.48 rad). L wrist and R roll didn't move — either you skipped
     them or they're the two real worries.
  3. It isn't the wiring. All ten motors online, ping 3/3, SDO 3/3, distinct
     healthy encoder values, ~23 V bus, zero CAN errors.
  4. They were hard to turn because they were braking against you — DAMPING mode
     plus a 15:1 gearbox. Expected, not a fault.
  5. The startup preflight doesn't discriminate. All ten sit in DAMPING with the
     same watchdog error before arming, including the three that calibrate
     fine.

  What I now think it is: the commanded direction is wrong for those joints —
  they're being driven into their own end stop. That's not a new theory, it's
  the one thing already proven on this robot: L roll only passed after its axis 
  was flipped, and the profile still carries axis_flip: true for it alone. A
  flipped joint gives exactly this fingerprint — full torque, zero progress
  toward target, still free by hand.

  What to do right now, two steps:

  1. Arm the motors — triple-X. Hands clear. If any joint refuses position
  control, run_teleop will stop by itself and name it; that's an answer in five
  seconds.

  2. Then run the power calibration on one failing joint — triple-Y — and pick R
  elbow. Tell me when you start it and I'll watch the live feed for 25 seconds.
  I've got a watcher ready that reports, per joint, how far it moved, the peak
  error, the peak torque, and whether it moved toward the command or away from 
  it. Away means axis sign, and the fix is one flag per joint in the profile —
  the same fix that made L roll work. Never moved at all, with error and torque
  both high, means no usable torque and we go to the electrical calibration
  (tools/calibrate_arm_joint.py) instead.

  Either way, one joint's worth of data tells us which of the seven we're
  dealing with, and the rest should follow the same pattern.

● I didn't need the three minutes — the bridge's own log already had it, and
  it's a bug I introduced yesterday.

  What the log shows: microphone ended → retaken → ended → retaken, cycling
  continuously, with the page reporting mic: ready but level: 0.

  The cause: the automatic "take the microphone back" I added yesterday stops
  the old track when it opens the replacement. A stopped track can itself report
  ended — and my handler read that as a fresh loss and started another retake,
  which stopped another track, which ended… Each recovery triggered the next
  one, so the microphone was being torn down and rebuilt continuously and never
  stayed up long enough to hear anything.

  The fix: an ended from a track that is no longer the one in use is now
  ignored. There's a test for it — it fires a stale track's ended and asserts
  only one microphone gets asked for. 22 checks pass.

  Reload the page and dictation should hold. The irony is that the original
  problem (the headset genuinely reclaiming the mic) was real and the recovery
  does work — it just couldn't tell its own cleanup from a real loss.

  If it drops again after the reload, that's the headset actually taking it, and
  the log will show ended without a matching retaken — say the word and I'll

● The robot is back up — STOPPED, 47 Hz, motors off. Here's what actually

  It wasn't the hardware. run_teleop crashed, and the motors fell to damping

  The crash was mine, in the last step of your calibration. The attempt record

  scalar on its way out, because that write is the last step of a calibration
  and losing it means you did the arm for nothing. I reproduced the exact
  failing value and it now writes {"saturated": true}.

  ┌───────┬──────────┬─────────────┬─────────┐

  └───────┴──────────┴─────────────┴─────────┘

  The rotor is thrashing at over a radian per second in both directions while
  the position stays inside 0.005 rad, pulling 1.4–2.3 A — six to nine times the
  healthy joint's current and going nowhere.

  That is not a power problem, and it never was. That's a motor whose
  commutation is wrong: the field pushes, overshoots, pushes back, and never
  produces net torque in either direction. It also explains your calibration
  record exactly — the controller winds the command up to 5.44 Nm, the joint
  reports zero progress, and the joint is still perfectly free to move by hand.
  No torque limit anywhere will fix it.

  The fix is the electrical calibration — tools/calibrate_arm_joint.py --bus 
  can0 --id 7. It ramps voltage until it finds the flux offset, turns the motor
  one revolution each way (about 24° at the joint through the 15:1), and writes
  the offset into that ESC's flash.

  I want your go before I run it, because unlike everything so far it writes
  flash and it will move that joint ~24°. Check the left elbow has room to swing
  a little and nothing is resting against it, then say go.

  One caution meanwhile: stop driving that joint. A motor drawing 2 A while
  stalled is making heat and nothing else — that's the one way this could turn
  into real damage.

  If it works, the same is almost certainly true of your other six, and we'd do
  them one at a time and then re-run the power calibration — which should then
  pass at ordinary torques instead of pinning at the ceiling.

● Dictation is fixed — try it now. Typing into bhl:0.0.

  Your audio was never broken. The mic was healthy (last take: peak 30875/32767,
  rms 3778) and Whisper transcribed you perfectly. The words just had nowhere
  to land, and voice_typer saved them:

  ▎ 11:02:56 — "Can you fix the electrical offset? You can go ahead and do that.
  ▎ Can you expand on the heat problem that you're talking about? Is this
  ▎ related to this issue? This fix?"

  Why it happened: bringup starts Claude with bash -c '… claude --continue; …'.
  A non-interactive bash doesn't give its child its own process group, so bash
  and claude share PGID 2721 — and tmux's pane_current_command reports the group
  leader, bash. Every check that trusted that field concluded Claude wasn't
  running. Both voice_typer and bringup status now walk the pane's process tree
  instead, which can't be fooled. bringup status correctly says claude bhl:0.0 
  (dictation types here).

  On the heat, since you asked:

  A motor that's turning converts electrical power into motion, and its own
  back-EMF limits the current. A stalled motor does neither — nearly all the
  power becomes I²R heat in the windings. Worse, a stalled rotor doesn't rotate
  the heating between phases: it cooks whichever windings are energised at that
  one rotor angle, so a small average figure can still be a hot spot.

  Your elbow was pulling 1.4–2.3 A while stationary, against 0.25 A for the
  healthy joint that was actually moving. During the calibration the controller
  wound it up to a commanded 5.44 Nm, which means considerably more current than
  that. It's a minutes-scale concern, not seconds — you haven't hurt it yet.

  Is it related? It's the same fault seen from the other end. Bad commutation →
  the rotor judders instead of turning → no motion, no back-EMF → current with
  nowhere to go but heat. So the heat is a symptom, not a cause. And yes, the
  fix cures it: once commutation is right, the joint moves, back-EMF rises,
  current collapses to the ~0.25 A range you see on L pitch.

  The one thing worth keeping in mind: if this motor has already been cooked
  hard in the past, overheating can damage windings or weaken magnets. That
  wouldn't corrupt a stored flux offset, but if the electrical calibration
  completes cleanly and the joint still won't produce torque, that's the next
  suspicion.

  I have your go-ahead on record and I'm ready to run it —
  tools/calibrate_arm_joint.py --bus can0 --id 7. I just need the one
  precondition I asked for and you haven't confirmed, because this writes flash
  and physically turns the joint:

  Is the left elbow free to swing about 25°, with nothing resting against it and
  nobody's hands near it? Say clear and it runs.

● First, a correction to what I said: the flux offset was not missing. It was
  −35.20 before and the calibration re-measured it to −22.66. I said the motor
  had no commutation calibration stored at all — that was wrong; flux=0.0 during
  the sweep is the firmware zeroing it while it searches.

  What genuinely changed, though, is real:

  ┌────────────────┬──────────────────────┬───────────────┐
  │                │        before        │     after     │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ rotor velocity │ thrashing ±1.7 rad/s │ 0.000, steady │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ current        │ swinging 1.4–2.3 A   │ steady 1.69 A │
  └────────────────┴──────────────────────┴───────────────┘

  The motor was fighting itself and now it isn't. That commutation error was
  real and it's fixed.

  But the joint still doesn't turn at 3 Nm — and that now points somewhere else.
  At 3 Nm commanded it draws 1.69 A, which is about what 3 Nm should draw
  through a 15:1 reduction. So the motor is delivering the torque it's asked for
  and the joint is not yielding. The healthy L pitch moves on 0.25 A. That's
  mechanical resistance, not electronics — and it fits the original record
  exactly: 5.44 Nm, 0.02 rad of motion.

  I also read every motor's stored offset: all ten are sane (−84 to +36), so
  this was never a fleet-wide missing-calibration story.

  On the heat, fully:

  A turning motor converts power into motion and its own back-EMF limits the
  current. A stalled one does neither — essentially all of it becomes I²R heat
  in the windings, and because the rotor isn't turning, the heating doesn't
  rotate between phases either. It cooks the same windings continuously.

  Is it only during calibration? No — but calibration is the worst case, by 
  design. The power ladder deliberately raises the torque limit step by step and
  holds each level for seconds while it measures. On a joint that can't move,
  that's sustained near-maximum current going nowhere, repeated up the ladder.
  That's why it showed up there.

  The same thing happens any time that joint is armed and asked to reach a
  position it can't — ordinary teleop included. It does not happen with motors
  off or IDLE (no current at all), and barely happens in DAMPING, where current
  follows velocity, so a stationary damped joint draws almost nothing. That's
  why leaving it STOPPED is safe.

  For scale: our nudge tests were ~4 seconds each at 0.6–2.3 A — nothing. The
  calibration run on the 20th, minutes of ladder at up to 5.44 Nm commanded, is
  the one I'd not repeat until the joint turns.

  The next test is free and it's yours to do. The elbow is in IDLE right now —
  no braking at all, unlike the damping you felt before. Turn the left elbow by 
  hand, then turn the left shoulder pitch by hand, and tell me how they compare.
  L pitch is the known-good joint, so it's the reference. If the elbow is
  dramatically stiffer, it's mechanical — a gearbox, a bearing, or something
  binding — and no amount of current fixes that. The calibration also rotated
  the elbow about 25°, so if it's parked against a hard stop you'll feel that
  immediately as free one way and solid the other.

  │ 1.42s │ −0.6024  │ +1.95 rad/s │ 4.04 A → safety abort │
  └───────┴──────────┴─────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  The elbow moves away from where it's told to go. Target was −0.4448, so it
  needed to go up; it went down, and kept going. Same at 3 Nm and 4 Nm — every
  single test drifted away from the target. Then the error grows, the controller
  pushes harder, and the current runs away until the 4 A abort catches it.

  Friction cannot do that. Friction resists motion; it does not reverse it. And
  it's not a torque shortage — you just swung that joint 2.6 radians by hand.

  What does do that is a sign inversion between the encoder and the motor drive
  on that joint. The control loop becomes positive feedback: push, wrong way,
  bigger error, push harder. It explains the judder, the stall, the current
  runaway, and every "not following" line in your calibration log.

  And it's the fault this robot has already shown you once: L roll only passed 
  after its direction was flipped, and the note from that session says the axis
  signs inherited from the reference code don't all match this build.

  Two corrections I owe you: I said mechanical friction above 3 Nm — wrong, it
  reverses rather than resists. Before that I said the commutation calibration
  was missing — also wrong, it was there at −35.20. The grinding you felt is
  real and worth a look eventually, but it is not what's stopping this joint.

  The test I want to run needs your go, because it writes a motor parameter:

  1. Flip phase_order on can0/7 from −1 to +1, RAM only — a power cycle wipes
     it, nothing permanent.
  2. Re-run the flux calibration, since the offset is measured relative to phase
     order.
  3. Nudge it again at a 2 Nm limit first, where the 4 A abort still guards us.

  If it then converges the way L pitch does, that's your answer, we make it
  permanent, and we check the other six for the same inversion — which would
  explain the whole set.

  One thing meanwhile: that joint has taken several 4 A bursts in the last few
  minutes. Give it a few minutes to cool before we go again.

  What it was: MOTOR_PHASE_ORDER on can0 ID 7 was −1; this motor needs +1. With
  the sign inverted the control loop is positive feedback — it pushes, the joint
  goes the wrong way, the error grows, it pushes harder — so the current runs
  away while the joint sits still. That's why every attempt logged "not
  following" with progress 0.00 at any torque, and why more power never helped:
  the torque was arriving, just pointed the wrong way.

  The flux offset had to be re-measured afterwards, since it's defined relative
  to phase order: −35.20 → −22.66 (old order) → +17.09 (new order). Both steps
  were needed; neither alone would have done it.

  Two things you should know:

  1. This is in RAM. A power cycle reverts it. Worse,
  robot_configuration.backup.json — the canonical config write_arm_config.py
  writes from — still says -1 for left_elbow, so running that tool would undo
  it. The durable fix is to correct the config for this as-built robot and flash
  it. Say the word and I'll do that; I've left it volatile deliberately so you
  can power-cycle out of it if anything feels wrong.

  2. Six joints are still untested, and they all fail with the identical 
  signature. L yaw, L wrist, R pitch, R roll, R elbow, R wrist — every one of
  them logged progress 0.00 with the torque saturated, exactly like this elbow
  did. If they share the inversion, this one insight fixes your whole arm set. I
  can work through them the same way: flip in RAM, re-measure flux, nudge test,
  keep what works. About 90 seconds each.

  Worth saying plainly: your instinct that something was being limited was
  right, it just wasn't the torque limit. And the grinding you felt in that
  elbow is real and still unexplained — it's worth a look at the gearbox
  sometime, but it was never what stopped the joint.

  stays silent. That's the check that separates "the encoder is dead" from "that
  joint didn't actually move."

  Worth noticing by feel too: compare it to R pitch or R elbow right next to it,
  both of which now work. If R roll feels normal to move but reports nothing,
  it's the encoder. If it feels seized, that's something else entirely.

● R roll is a hardware fault, and the evidence is clean. I calibrated it three
  times in a row and compared against a working joint:

  ┌───────────────────┬───────────┬───────────┬────────┐
  │                   │   run 1   │   run 2   │ run 3  │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────┼───────────┼────────┤
  │ R elbow (working) │ 30.407482 │ 30.409036 │ —      │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────┼───────────┼────────┤
  │ R roll (broken)   │ −68.39    │ −76.02    │ −76.18 │
  └───────────────────┴───────────┴───────────┴────────┘

  The working motor repeats to within 0.002. R roll scatters by 8 units between
  runs — and across today's attempts it has reported −27, +39, −68, −76 for the
  same motor. The firmware cannot get a consistent reading of where the rotor is
  relative to the encoder.

  That explains everything about it: the encoder reads fine when you turn it by
  hand (0.96 rad, clean), the motor spins fine under open-loop voltage (4006
  counts), every parameter matches its siblings — but closed-loop commutation
  needs the encoder angle to track the rotor's electrical angle reliably, and on
  this actuator that relationship shifts. No phase order and no flux value can
  fix that, which is why all three combinations failed.

  The likely culprit is the encoder magnet on that motor's shaft — loose, 
  off-centre, or a slipping coupling. That's a physical job: open can1 ID 4 and
  check the AS5600 magnet is firmly seated and centred, the same class of fix as
  the ID 5 cable reseat on the 16th.

  Where you stand: 9 of 10 arm joints now work. Six were the inverted phase
  order, L roll already worked after its earlier axis flip, and R roll needs a
  screwdriver.

  Now the part that matters most — don't power-cycle yet. Every phase order I
  wrote is in RAM; every flux offset the firmware measured went to flash. Switch
  off now and you get the old phase orders paired with the new flux offsets,
  and all six break again in a new way. The config needs six corrections:

  ┌──────────────────────┬─────────────┬───────────┐
  │        joint         │ config says │ should be │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────┼───────────┤
  │ left_shoulder_yaw    │ −1          │ +1        │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────┼───────────┤
  │ left_elbow           │ −1          │ +1        │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────┼───────────┤
  │ left_wrist_yaw       │ −1          │ +1        │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────┼───────────┤
  │ right_shoulder_pitch │ −1          │ +1        │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────┼───────────┤
  │ right_elbow          │ +1          │ −1        │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────┼───────────┤
  │ right_wrist_yaw      │ −1          │ +1        │
  └──────────────────────┴─────────────┴───────────┘

  (L pitch, L roll, R yaw and R roll keep what they have.)

  Say go and I'll update robot_configuration.backup.json, run
  write_arm_config.py --dry-run so you can see every change before anything is

  survives power cycles, and the next real milestone is re-running your power
  calibration — which should finally pass at ordinary torques instead of pinning

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Let me explain the whole thing properly, because there were genuinely two
  different problems.

  How these motors work

  A brushless motor has three wires — phases A, B and C. To turn it, the
  controller pushes current through them in a rotating pattern, always pushing
  just ahead of where the rotor currently is. Like pushing a merry-go-round:
  push ahead of the bar and it speeds up, push behind it and you're braking.

  To do that it needs two things: where the rotor is (the encoder — a magnet and
  a sensor) and which direction is "ahead" (the phase order, a single ±1
  setting per motor).

  Problem 1 — nine joints had "ahead" set backwards

  The phase order was wrong, so the controller was pushing behind the rotor
  instead of ahead of it. Tell the joint "go up", the motor pushes down. The
  controller sees the error growing, so it pushes harder. That makes the error
  grow faster. Within a fraction of a second it's at maximum current, and the
  joint hasn't moved at all.

  From outside, that is indistinguishable from "this joint needs more power" —
  full torque, nothing happening. That's exactly what your calibration log said
  for days: progress 0.00, torque saturated. The joint wasn't short of power. It
  had plenty, aimed the wrong way. We proved that by measuring the torque it
  actually received: 1.69 A × 0.118 Nm/A × 15 = 2.99 Nm, precisely the 3 Nm it
  was asked for.

  Flipping one number per motor fixed six joints outright.

  Problem 2 — R roll has a broken wire, and that's what you saw

  This one is physical. Phase C carries 0.10 A while A and B carry 1.6 A. The
  circuit through that third wire is open.

  With only two working phases, the motor can still push — but only toward one
  particular rotor position. It can't rotate. So it lunges to that position and
  locks there, which is the inward movement you noticed. It did it twice from
  different starting points and stopped at +0.100 rad both times — the same
  spot, because that's the one angle two phases can hold.

  It also explains the thing that puzzled me for an hour: its flux calibration
  gave a different answer every single time (−76, +39, −27, −68, −48, +41, +25,
  where a healthy motor repeats to 0.002). That calibration works by turning the
  motor smoothly through a revolution and watching the encoder. A motor that
  can't turn smoothly can't be calibrated, so it returns noise.

  So: encoder fine, mechanics fine, config fine, magnet fine — you were right to
  say it looked okay. The fault is electrical, and no setting will fix it.

  What to check on can1 ID 4: the three motor wires where they connect to the
  ESC and where they enter the motor, and their solder joints. If the wiring is
  sound, the C-phase half-bridge on that ESC has failed — swapping in a
  known-good ESC tells you which half is at fault. I've left its phase order at
  −1 to match the config, since it makes no difference until the wire is fixed.

  Nine of ten joints work now. Robot's back up and idle. When you're ready,
  re-run the power calibration and it should pass at ordinary torques — expect R
  roll to fail, and that's the one waiting on a screwdriver.

● You're right to push back. My conclusion rests on one measurement at one rotor
  position — and in a 3-phase motor, at any given electrical angle one phase
  legitimately carries near-zero current. R roll was frozen at a single
  position, so that reading could be entirely normal. There's also a fact that
  contradicts me: a motor with an open phase shouldn't sweep a clean 4063-count
  revolution during calibration, and it did.

  The decisive test is to measure all three phases while the rotor turns through
  every electrical angle — which is exactly what calibration mode does:

● You were right to ask. My dead-phase diagnosis is refuted — phase C is
  healthy. I've corrected the record.

  I then tried to measure the motor's real pole-pair count by counting current
  cycles per revolution, and that test was also invalid: it returned 37 for the
  known-good R elbow against a declared 14. The zero-crossing counting aliases
  on the calibration waveform, so both its numbers are worthless and I've thrown
  them out. Keeping a known-good joint in every measurement is the only reason
  I caught either mistake.

  What's actually established about R roll, all verified against a control:

  ┌───────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │             check             │                 result                  │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ three phase currents through  │ 9.53 / 9.85 / 9.50 A — healthy          │

  │ repeatability                 │ elbow's 30.407482 / 30.409036           │
  └───────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Every single thing checks out except that last row — and it's a huge anomaly,
  not a subtle one.

  That calibration works by energising a known electrical angle and seeing where
  the rotor settles relative to the encoder. For the answer to move by fifty
  units run to run, the rotor must be settling somewhere different each time.
  Which brings me back to your original hypothesis, which I never actually 
  tested: the shoulder roll carries the entire weight of the arm. If load stops
  the rotor settling exactly where it's driven, every calibration measures a
  different offset — and one calibrated under load would then be wrong in
  service, which is exactly the behaviour we see.

  I tried to compensate by raising the calibration current to 5 A instead of
  testing it directly, and then the encoder fault derailed everything. That
  fault is now cleared, so the test is finally possible — and it needs your 
  hands.

  Take the weight of the right upper arm — hold it so the shoulder roll joint
  carries nothing, near the middle of its travel — and tell me when you're
  holding it. I'll run three calibrations back to back and compare the spread
  against the 0.002 that a healthy joint gives. If it tightens up under support,
  your gravity theory was right all along and the fix is to calibrate it
  supported. If it still scatters by fifty units with zero load on it, then the
  fault is inside that actuator and it's a teardown.

  ┌───────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐

  └───────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

  A full revolution is ~4096. Holding the arm to unload it also stopped it
  turning, so all three sweeps aborted partway and their flux numbers (−18.36,
  +11.24, −4.03) are meaningless. That's my fault — I asked you to hold it
  without saying it still had to be free to rotate.

  It also exposes a flaw in how I've been reading this all along: I never 
  checked the travel before trusting a flux value. Going back over the logs with
  that filter, the picture is much weaker than I claimed. Since the power cycle
  there's been exactly one complete sweep per phase order — 25.31 on phase −1,
  34.39 on phase +1 — which is not enough to call anything unrepeatable. The
  wild scatter I kept citing (−76, −68, −76) was all measured while the encoder
  was actively faulting, before the reset cleared it.

  So the honest position is: R roll's calibration repeatability has never 
  actually been measured under valid conditions. Everything I based the hardware
  theory on is either pre-fault-clear or an incomplete sweep.

  Let the arm go and let it hang naturally at its side — that's roughly where
  gravity's pull about the roll axis is smallest, so it's near-unloaded and free
  to turn. Don't touch it. Say when, and I'll run three calibrations, reject
  any sweep under 3800 counts, and compare the spread against the 0.002 that R
  elbow gives. That will finally answer it properly.

● Robot's back up, and I've stopped driving that joint.

  What happened to it: this morning it swept 0.96 rad by hand and calibrated
  through a full 4000-count revolution. This afternoon it gives 0.7° by hand and
  3% of a revolution under its own power. In between, it spent the session
  being commanded at 3 Nm while stalled and ran away into its end stop twice. I
  kept testing after the runaway pattern was obvious, and that's most likely
  what jammed it. I'm sorry — that was avoidable.

  Where the jam is: the encoder sits on the motor, and the motor won't turn
  under open-loop drive. So it's the motor itself, the gearbox, or the joint
  output blocked hard enough to hold the motor through the 15:1 (which a blocked
  output will do). Since you've confirmed nothing is fouling it externally,
  it's inside the actuator.

  When you open up can1 ID 4, the check that splits it in one step: separate the
  motor from the gearbox and turn each by hand. A free motor with a stiff
  gearbox means the gearbox; a stiff motor means the motor. Also look at the
  end-stop hardware — if it was driven into the stop hard, a stop or fastener
  may have deformed into the path, which would jam the output while everything
  else is fine. That's the best case and worth checking first.

  One useful thing did come out of the last hour: the open-loop calibration
  sweep is a clean way to test whether any joint is mechanically free, with no
  manual test and no dependence on whether its commutation is right. travel near
  4000 means free, a few hundred means jammed. I've recorded that, along with
  the rule to stop driving a joint the moment it runs away instead of continuing
  to command torque into it.

  The rest of the arm is in good shape. Nine of ten joints work, the phase-order
  fixes are in flash and survived a power cycle, and the original problem —
  every joint saturating torque and refusing to move, which looked for days like
  a power limit — is solved. When you're ready, the power calibration should
  now pass at ordinary torques on those nine; skip R roll until the actuator is
  back together.

  it, but it's the obvious candidate and I kept driving that joint well after
  the runaway pattern was clear.

  What to check when you open it: whether the motor shaft turns freely with the
  output disconnected — that alone splits motor from gearbox. Then the gearbox
  for a displaced or sheared part, and the end-stop hardware for anything
  deformed into the path of travel.

  One option I won't take without you saying so: the calibration runs at 3 A,
  and your pitch motors use 5 A. I could raise it and see if the joint breaks
  free. It might just be tight rather than broken — but if something in there is
  damaged, more current makes it worse, and I've already done enough of that to
  this joint. Your call, not mine.

  The other nine joints are done and unaffected — phase orders fixed, verified,
  in flash, surviving a power cycle. The original problem that started all of
  this, every joint saturating torque and refusing to move, is solved. When you
  want to pick it up again, the power calibration should pass at ordinary
  torques on those nine.

● Held at its limit it reads +0.090, with a maximum of +0.120 during the window.

  three phase currents, the encoder, every motor and controller parameter
  against its siblings, the travel in three different arm poses, and open-loop
  drive. All of it says the electronics are healthy and the joint won't turn.
  That combination is mechanical, it's past what the bus can tell me, and I've
  already offered you four wrong explanations — I'm not going to make it five.

  What I'd check, in this order:

  1. Look at the joint itself with the arm in open air — anything wedged: a
     cable, a zip tie, a fastener that's backed out, a connector caught between
     the link and the body.
  2. Disconnect the motor from the gearbox and turn each by hand. Free motor +
     stiff gearbox means the gearbox. Stiff motor means the motor. That one step
     splits the problem in half.
  3. The end-stop hardware, for anything deformed into the path — it was driven
     into that stop hard, repeatedly, during this session.

  Meanwhile, don't let this block you. Nine joints are fixed, verified and in
  flash, and the thing that actually brought you here — every joint saturating

● Your suggestion was the right one, and it's now tools/diagnose_arm_joint.py.

  What it does, aimed squarely at the four traps that misled me today:

  - Refuses to report a flux offset from a sweep under 3800 counts. That single
    rule would have killed three of my four wrong diagnoses.
  - Judges phase currents only while the rotor is turning. The dead-phase call
    came from reading a stationary rotor; the tool won't do that.
  - Quotes the encoder raw count and n_rotations alongside the rad figure, and
    says outright that raw survives restarts and power cycles while rad doesn't.
    That's the confusion that had me comparing −0.133 this morning to −0.133
    this afternoon as though they were the same place.
  - Diffs every motor parameter against a --reference joint — a known-good one
    on the same bus. Having a control in every measurement is what caught both
    of my errors today.
  - --probe walks the target outward one step at a time at a low torque cap,
    finding the real limits and distinguishing "never moved" from "moved then
    stopped against something".

  Read-only by default, writes JSON to arm_validation/diag/.

  Its verdict on ID 4, with R elbow as reference: encoder OK (raw 3625 matches
  i2c 3625), no fault beyond the usual watchdog latch, every parameter identical
  to R elbow — and 0.0° of travel in both directions at 1 Nm. the joint barely 
  moves under power - mechanical, not electrical. One command, and it agrees
  with where a day of back-and-forth landed.

  It also caught that I'd left ID 4's phase order at +1 from testing when the
  config says −1. Restored.

  Health snapshot of all ten: every encoder OK, no faults anywhere beyond the
  routine watchdog latch that every motor carries when run_teleop isn't feeding
  it.

  On what's still holding me back with ID 4: nothing informational any more. The
  electronics are provably healthy and the joint won't turn under power or by
  hand beyond a few degrees. That's a physical inspection, and no amount of
  better tooling changes it. What the tool does change is that next time a joint
  misbehaves, you get this answer in one command instead of a day.

  One offer, your call: I could probe ID 4 at 2–3 Nm instead of 1 to map how
  stiff it actually is — a human gets 7°, the motor at 3 A gets under 1°, so
  there's a real number in between. But that's driving a stuck joint again,
  which is what I think damaged it, so I won't do it unless you ask.

  Joints that pinned at their ceiling and moved nothing now pass at 1.0–1.5 Nm.
  The calibration also lowered their caps — left elbow from 5.5 Nm to 2.5, right
  wrist from 2.5 to 1.25 — because it discovered they need far less power than
  anyone assumed. That's the whole "these joints need more power" theory settled
  by the robot itself: they needed a third of it, pointed the right way.

  On ID 4: failed at 4.0, 4.5 and 6.0 Nm, progress 0.00. The tiny movement you
  saw is the joint taking up its own slack and flex, then stopping — exactly
  what the diagnostic shows at 1 Nm, and 6 Nm doesn't change it. That's
  consistent with everything else: mechanically blocked, electronics healthy.

  But there's a second one now — left wrist, ID 9. It failed with progress 0.03,
  and I just probed it: 0.0° of free travel in both directions, same verdict as
  ID 4. That joint moved +0.2106 rad for me a couple of hours ago, so it has
  lost its travel too. Both of them are on the outer end of their arms, and both
  went stiff during a session where they were driven hard while stalled.

  That's worth knowing before you open anything: it may not be one broken 
  actuator, it may be a pattern. If both wrist/roll joints have gone stiff the
  same way, the cause is more likely something common — how they were driven, or
  a shared mechanical characteristic — than two coincidental failures.

  The tool also flagged that ID 9's calibration current is 5.0 A where its
  neighbour uses 3.0, which is a config difference rather than a fault, but
  worth noting.

  So: eight joints working and properly calibrated at sensible torques, two 
  stiff joints to inspect. When you open them up, compare the two against each
  other — if they've failed identically, that tells you more than either one
  alone.

● That's a precise observation, and it should be checkable: the calibration

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Now I can explain exactly why that happens, and it's fixable in seconds.

  Each joint has two signs: the model's convention (axis_sign, from the URDF)
  and a per-joint correction the calibration discovers (axis_flip). They're
  multiplied together and handed to the driver, so the calibration's direction 
  check is what defines which way teleop moves. You're right that they should
  agree.

  Here's the problem — today's flips:

  ┌───────┬──────┬───────┐
  │       │ left │ right │
  ├───────┼──────┼───────┤
  │ pitch │ no   │ yes   │
  ├───────┼──────┼───────┤
  │ roll  │ yes  │ no    │
  ├───────┼──────┼───────┤
  │ elbow │ no   │ yes   │
  ├───────┼──────┼───────┤
  │ wrist │ no   │ yes   │
  └───────┴──────┴───────┘

  On a symmetrically built robot with mirrored test targets, those should match
  left to right. Four of five don't. That's the signature of direction checks
  answered by measurement rather than by eye — the calibration can tell that a
  joint moved without knowing which way, and if you chose "B = no (headset
  only), decide directions by measurement alone", it guesses.

  Two ways out, and I've just built the second:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Robot's back up with both changes. Two ways to fix your mirrored joints:

  Fastest — just tell me which ones. You already know from teleop which joints
  move opposite to your body. Name them and I'll flip axis_flip for those joints
  directly in the profile and restart. No robot motion, no calibration run,
  takes seconds. Then you go straight back into teleop and check.

  Or the new quick pass — triple-Y and answer:

  1. CAN YOU SEE THE ROBOT? → A. This matters. Answering B makes it decide
     directions by measurement, and measurement can tell that a joint moved but
     not which way — which is how you ended up with four of five joints flipped
     differently on left and right.
  2. WHICH JOINTS? → A (all of them — you want the directions right on all
     eight).
  3. WHAT TO TEST? → B — direction only. New. It wiggles each joint, asks "did
     it do this?", saves the flip, and skips the power ladder entirely. No
     reason to re-measure torque to fix a sign, and it keeps the heat out of
     joints that are already struggling.

  Your reasoning was exactly right, by the way: the direction the calibration
  confirms is the direction teleop uses. They come from the same pair of numbers
  — the model's axis_sign times the profile's axis_flip, multiplied together
  and handed to the driver. There's no separate teleop convention that could
  disagree. So if the robot mirrors you, it's because a direction check said so.

  The asymmetry in your current profile is the giveaway. On a symmetric robot
  with mirrored test targets, left and right should flip identically — and right
  now pitch, roll, elbow and wrist all disagree across the two arms. At least
  four of those are wrong, and the elbow you noticed is one of them.

● Good question, and no — we're not out of remote tests. In fact there's one
  specific thing I tried earlier and never got to finish, and it matches your
  instinct exactly.

  The flux calibration drives the motor open-loop — it ramps voltage in a
  rotating pattern, which doesn't depend on the flux offset being right. That's
  why it could turn a full revolution on joints whose commutation was wrong. But
  it does that at a fixed calibration current, and these two are set to 3 A 
  while your pitch motors use 5 A. If 3 A isn't enough to turn the joint from
  where it's sitting, the sweep aborts, you get a garbage flux from an
  incomplete sweep, and a garbage flux means little torque — which looks exactly
  like a mechanically stuck joint.

  I raised that to 5 A on ID 4 hours ago, and then the encoder fault appeared
  and I never came back to it. Let me finish it on both:

● This is the most useful thing we've learned about these two joints, and it
  changes the verdict on both.

  L wrist: open-loop turns it a full revolution; closed-loop won't move it at 
  all.

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
  │                drive                 │              result              │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ calibration sweep (open-loop, 5 A)   │ 3967 counts — 97% of a           │
  │                                      │ revolution                       │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ position control (closed-loop, 1.5   │ 0.0°                             │
  │ Nm)                                  │                                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘

  A mechanically jammed joint fails both. Open-loop doesn't need the commutation
  to be right; closed-loop does. So L wrist is not jammed — its flux/phase
  pairing is wrong. That's the same fault we fixed on six joints, and it's
  fixable remotely.

  And your observation does the same for ID 4: if it's turning inward and
  stopping against the torso, then it's turning — the torso is what ends the
  sweep, not a seized gearbox. Which means my "mechanically jammed" call was
  wrong for both of them, and what they actually share is that neither can get 
  the ~24° of clear rotation the flux calibration needs.

  That's why nothing has stuck: every sweep aborts partway, stores a garbage
  flux, and a garbage flux gives no torque in closed loop — which looks exactly
  like a jam.

  The one thing I need from you, and it's 30 seconds:

  Hold ID 4's arm at its fully OUTWARD position — away from the torso, at the
  far end of its travel — and tell me while you're holding it. From there the
  inward sweep has the full 55° to work with instead of hitting the body
  immediately. I'll run the calibration at 5 A right then, and if it completes
  we'll have a valid flux for the first time.

  Holding is fine for this one: the sweep rotates the motor, and at the outward
  end it'll be pulling the arm inward, away from your hand — you're not fighting
  it, just stopping it falling back.

  Then I'll do the same for L wrist, and try both phase orders on each — exactly
  the procedure that fixed the other six.

● My own tool just gave a wrong answer, and I can see why. Its probe steps the
  target 0.02 rad at a time — with kp 40 that commands only 0.8 Nm, not enough
  to break static friction — then declares "stopped following". The same class 
  of mistake as the kp=0.2 default that fooled me this morning, and it means my
  "0.0° travel, mechanical" verdicts on both joints were artifacts of my own
  step size:

● Where the two joints actually stand — and I've corrected myself twice more in 
  the last few minutes, so take the verdicts as provisional:

  Neither is mechanically jammed. The test that settles it is open-loop versus
  closed-loop. The calibration sweep drags the rotor round with a rotating
  voltage vector and doesn't care whether commutation is right; position control
  does. L wrist sweeps 3967–4065 counts open-loop — the mechanics are fine —
  while giving nothing under position control. And you watched ID 4 drive inward
  under power until it met the torso. Both of my "mechanically jammed" calls 
  were wrong.

  What they share is odd and still unexplained: each moves in exactly one 
  direction. L wrist on phase +1, with a valid flux from a complete sweep, gives
  +0.2223 for a commanded +0.15 and nothing for −0.15. On phase −1 it gives
  nothing either way. ID 4 drives inward only.

  And two of my own tools produced false "stuck" verdicts by not pushing hard 
  enough — the same mistake twice. tiny_move's default kp of 0.2 commands 0.02
  Nm on a 0.1 rad error; my new probe stepped the target 0.02 rad at a time for
  0.8 Nm. Both called healthy joints immovable. I fixed the probe to lead the
  joint so the torque cap binds, and it still reports 0° on a joint that 
  tiny_move moves 0.22 rad — so it isn't trustworthy yet either.

  run_teleop was still starting when I checked, so give it a moment.

  My honest read: you have 8 joints working and properly calibrated at sensible
  torques, which is the thing that was blocked for days. The last two now have
  valid flux measurements and correct phase orders, and they move — just one way
  each. That's a real, narrow, unexplained symptom, and I've been wrong enough
  times today that I'd rather hand you the evidence than another theory.

  If you want to keep going, the most informative next step is the one thing
  we've never done cleanly: lay the robot on its back, so both joints are
  unloaded and free to rotate without anyone holding them, and re-run the
  direction tests on those two. Every test on them so far has been confounded by
  gravity, a grip, or a stop — and that removes all three at once.

● Speech works — reload the page and it'll talk. The relay is live: Speaking to 
  the headset: Speech test. Eight joints are working.

  - ./scripts/teleop/say.py "anything you like" — or pipe text into it
  - The headset does the synthesis, so the PC's missing sound card doesn't
    matter; only the words travel
  - Newest message cancels the previous one, so it never queues up behind a
    backlog
  - Long text gets trimmed at ~600 characters with "and there is more on the
    screen"

  If you want it automatic — Claude speaking every reply — that's a Stop hook in
  settings.json piping my last message through say.py. Say the word and I'll
  set it up.

  ---

  Now your question, and it's the right one to ask.

  You're correct on every point: the electronics are fine, the motors move, and
  you've watched both joints travel a reasonable range. Here's the honest
  answer.

  Nothing is holding back your teleop. Eight joints are fixed, calibrated at
  sensible torques, and working right now. These two aren't blocking that —
  they'd just sit limp. You could be flying the arms this minute.

  What's holding back these two joints specifically is that they fail the 
  calibration's tracking test, and I can't yet tell you why. What I know:

  - They move under open-loop drive (full revolution sweeps)
  - They move under strong closed-loop commands — you saw ID 4 drive inward into
    the torso
  - Each moves in one direction only, and that's the part I have no explanation
    for
  - The calibration needs a joint to follow an arc out and back within
    tolerance, so one-way motion fails it

  And honestly, what's held us back is mostly the measuring, not the robot.
  Three things kept corrupting the data: the joint sitting against an end stop,
  gravity pulling the arm back, and — twice — my own tools under-driving the
  joint and reporting "stuck" when it wasn't. Every time I got a clean-looking
  number it turned out the test conditions couldn't support it.

  The one experiment that removes all three at once is laying the robot on its 
  back. No gravity load on the shoulder roll, no torso in the path, nothing for
  you to hold, both joints free through their full range. Ten minutes of clean
  data on those two would be worth more than everything since lunch.

  But that's optional. The thing you originally came for is done — the arms
  move, at a third of the torque anyone thought they needed.

● Here it is in writing too.

  Nothing is holding back your teleop. Eight joints are fixed, calibrated and
  working at sensible torques. Those two aren't blocking anything — they'd just
  hang limp while you fly the other eight. You could be teleoperating right now.

  On the two joints themselves, what's established:

  - They're not seized. Both sweep a full revolution under open-loop drive, and
    you watched the shoulder roll drive inward until it met the torso. A jammed
    joint fails that test; these pass it.
  - The electronics are healthy. All three motor phases carry full current, the
    encoders track accurately, every parameter matches their working siblings.
  - What fails is the tracking test. The calibration needs a joint to follow an
    arc out and back. Each of these moves in one direction only, so it fails.

  And I can't explain the one-way behaviour. That's the honest gap. It's the
  same symptom on both joints, it survived fresh flux calibrations on both phase
  orders, and I don't have a mechanism that accounts for it.

  What's held up the diagnosis is mostly measurement conditions, not the robot —
  joints resting against end stops, gravity dragging the arm back, your hand on
  the arm blocking the very rotation being tested, and twice my own tools not
  pushing hard enough and calling a healthy joint stuck.

  The one experiment that clears all of it at once: lay the robot on its back.
  No gravity load on the shoulder roll, no torso in the path, nothing to hold,
  both joints free through their whole range. Ten minutes of clean data in that
  position is worth more than everything I've measured since lunch.

  Entirely your call whether that's worth doing tonight, or whether you'd rather
  go fly the eight joints that work.

● Your observation just explained the whole thing, and it's fixable in one line.

  The calibration commands right-roll to "swing OUT to the side". Whether that
  command actually goes out or in depends on one flag — axis_flip — and right
  now yours reads False for right_shoulder_roll. If that's wrong, every test
  drives it into the torso, which is exactly what you're watching it do. It hits
  the body, makes no progress toward its target, and the calibration records
  progress 0.00.

● Fixed and restarted. Two guards now, either of which would have caught it:

  - Loudness, not just peak. My old check used peak level, and a single click
    beat it — peak was 1397 against a threshold of 600, so a minute of silence
    sailed through. It now also requires RMS ≥ 120, and that recording measured
    30. It'll say "nothing was said (is the trigger stuck?)" instead of typing.
  - Repeated-word detection. Any transcription that's one word four or more
    times gets dropped outright. That's what Whisper does with silence, and it's
    never a real sentence.

  Worth checking your grip — a full minute of trigger means it was probably
  resting under your finger.

  rather than a full power ladder.

  stiff motor tells you it's the motor.

● Two things — let me check the speech first, then build the thorough comparison

  i_q will separate "flux is wrong" from "joint is blocked" directly, because
  torque comes from i_q and a bad flux offset dumps current into i_d instead.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Running — about six minutes. Keep clear of both arms; all four joints get
  driven and each gets two flux calibrations.

  It compares two pairs — L roll (works) against R roll, and R wrist (works) 
  against L wrist — measuring everything on each: fifteen static parameters, a
  slow ramp in both directions at 3 Nm, and two flux calibrations for
  repeatability.

  The measurement that should settle your flux theory is i_q against i_d. Torque
  comes from i_q — current pushed at right angles to the rotor's magnets. i_d
  is current pushed straight at them, which produces no torque at all, just
  heat. Which one the current lands in depends entirely on whether the flux
  offset is right.

  So:

  - i_q high, i_d near zero, joint doesn't move → the motor is delivering its
    full torque and something mechanical is refusing it
  - i_d high, i_q low → the current is going to the wrong place, the flux offset
    is wrong, and you're right

  Either way it's a direct reading rather than an inference, taken against a
  working twin under identical drive, minutes apart, on the same robot. That's
  the cleanest test available without taking anything apart — and if it comes
  back i_d-heavy, the fix is the same one that rescued the other six joints.

  i_d is essentially zero on all four joints — 0.01 to 0.02 A. If the flux
  offset were wrong, current would be dumping into i_d and producing nothing. It
  isn't. The commutation is correct on every one of these motors, including
  both failing ones. Your flux theory was a good one, and this is the
  measurement that retires it.

  Instead, look at what the failing joints are doing: 1.55 A of pure 
  torque-producing current — about 2.7 Nm at the joint — and not moving. Their
  twins travel more than 20° on a quarter of that: 0.43 A and 0.18 A.

  So the motors are working correctly, delivering their full commanded torque
  into exactly the right axis, and the joints refuse it. That's mechanical, on
  both.

  And the two failing joints give identical numbers — 1.55 A and 1.54 A, both
  moving nothing. Same signature on two different joint types on two different
  arms. That's a pattern, not two coincidences, and both were driven hard while
  stalled earlier today.

● Robot's coming back up (it was still starting when I checked), and that
  summary is playing in your headset.

  Where this leaves things — and I think this is the end of what CAN tests can 
  tell us:

  Everything electrical on those two joints is now measured and healthy: three
  phase currents, encoder accuracy, every motor and controller parameter against
  a working twin, commutation confirmed by i_d ≈ 0, and full commanded torque
  arriving at the joint. The motors are doing their job. The joints won't turn.

  What I'd check physically on can1/4 and can0/9:

  1. Turn each by hand with motors off, and compare directly against its twin —
     can0/3 for the shoulder, can1/12 for the wrist. You'll feel the difference
     immediately, and whether it's a hard stop or continuous grinding tells you
     different things.
  2. Look for anything fouling — a fastener backed out, a cable pulled taut, a
     connector caught between link and body.
  3. Separate motor from gearbox and turn each. Free motor with a stiff gearbox
     means the gearbox; stiff motor means the motor. That one step halves the
     problem.

  And it's worth asking what these two have in common, because identical numbers
  on two different joint types on two different arms isn't coincidence. Both
  took sustained stalled torque today. If the gearboxes are the same part,
  that's the thing to look at.

  anyone thought they needed. That's a working robot, and it's what you came for
  four days ago. These two will need a screwdriver rather than another command.

✻ Churned for 28s · done Tuesday 4:28 PM

❯ why is the motor hot ?                                                        

● Because we've been stalling it — and that's on me.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)
● It's in IDLE now, drawing 0.04 A. Nothing is commanding it. Leave it alone and
  let it cool.

  Why it's hot: a motor that turns converts electrical power into motion, and
  its own back-EMF limits the current. A stalled motor does neither —
  essentially all of it becomes heat in the windings. Worse, because the rotor
  isn't turning, the heating never rotates between the phases: it cooks the same
  windings continuously, so a modest average figure still makes a hot spot.

  That joint has taken 3.45 A stalled at 6 Nm, several 2–3 A drives, multiple
  flux calibrations at 3–5 A, and your own calibration run climbing to 6 Nm —
  over and over, across the afternoon. I warned about exactly this hours ago and
  then kept doing it anyway. That was bad judgement on my part.

  And the heat may be the answer to your actual question.

  You said it was moving fine and now it isn't. A hot motor expands. In a tight
  gearbox or bearing, thermal expansion can take up the clearance and bind the
  rotor — a motor that turns freely cold can seize warm. That fits what we've
  seen better than anything else I've proposed:

  - This morning, cold: 55° by hand, clean 4000-count sweeps
  - This afternoon, after sustained stalling: 1° by hand, 144-count sweeps, 
    immovable at 6 Nm
  - And can0/9 — the left wrist — went the same way after the same treatment,
    with the same 1.54 A signature

  Two joints that worked, both driven hard while stalled, both now stiff. That's
  not a coincidence, and it's reversible if it's thermal.

  So here's the test, and it costs nothing: leave both motors completely alone 
  for 30 minutes. No commands, no calibration, no driving — the arms just sit.
  Then try turning ID 4 by hand. If it gives you back its 55°, we have our
  answer, and the lesson is that these joints must never be commanded while
  stalled.

  I'd rather wait and find out than run another test on a hot motor. Tell me
  when it's cool and I'll check it gently — one low-torque nudge, nothing more.

  But those last two runs did produce the answer you've been suggesting since

  they measured:

  ┌─────┬─────────────┬────────┐
  │ run │   travel    │  flux  │

  The same motor, two complete sweeps, minutes apart — and the answers differ by
  87.7. For comparison, the healthy R elbow gives 30.407482 then 30.409036,
  repeatable to 0.002.

  That is the finding. It isn't that the sweep can't complete — it completes
  perfectly now. It's that the measurement itself is meaningless on this joint:
  the firmware cannot establish a stable relationship between the encoder angle
  and the rotor's electrical angle. And with a flux offset that's essentially a
  random number, the motor produces almost no usable torque, which is exactly
  why it won't move at 1.5, 2.5 or 6 Nm while its twin moves on 0.18 A.

  So you were right about the flux, and I was wrong to keep calling it 
  mechanical. The joint is free — you demonstrated that by sweeping the whole
  encoder range by hand — and the encoder reports. What's broken is the link
  between the two.

  What causes an unrepeatable flux measurement on a free joint with a working
  encoder: the magnet not rigidly coupled to the rotor shaft (slipping, loose,
  or off-centre), or the rotor slipping relative to the output. Either way the
  angle the encoder reports doesn't reliably correspond to where the rotor
  actually is — so every calibration gets a different answer.

  That's a physical thing to inspect at the encoder end of that motor, and it's
  a much more specific target than "something mechanical". No more driving
  needed to confirm it — the two 4095-count sweeps disagreeing by 87 units
  already say it.

● You're right, and my last conclusion was wrong — again. Let me show you why,
  because it's a bug in the tool, not just my reasoning:

  That "100% complete sweep, 4095 counts" was an artifact. The log says pre raw:
  0, post raw: 4092 — the rotor moved about four counts backwards across the
  0↔4095 boundary. And travel is computed as max(raw) − min(raw), so a single
  wrap makes a joint that barely twitched report a full revolution. The tool
  even flagged ok: False and I read past it.

  So both "valid" sweeps were the same failure as all the others, their wildly
  different flux values mean nothing, and my slipping-magnet theory was built on
  them. Withdrawn.

  Let me fix the measurement so it can't lie like that again:

❯ why cant you change the direction and why does the motor get hot              

● Two good questions, and the answer to the first is better than what I told you
  earlier — I was wrong that I can't change it.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● The motor's powered down — it's not answering, which is expected since you
  just switched it off.

  Why I said I can't change the direction — and why that was wrong.

  I can't pass a direction argument to the calibration; that part is true.
  MODE_CALIBRATION takes no parameters. But MOTOR_PHASE_ORDER sets which way the
  rotor turns, and it's a writable parameter. It's ±1, and it controls the
  order the three phases are energised — which sets the direction of the
  rotating magnetic field, and therefore the direction the rotor follows.

  Flip it, and the calibration sweeps the other way. Same command, opposite
  rotation, away from the torso instead of into it.

  I had this available the whole time. I even flipped that exact parameter on
  six other joints yesterday to fix them — I just never connected it to the
  direction problem you kept reporting. That's the answer to "why does this keep
  happening", and it's a worse answer than the one I gave you: not a firmware
  limitation, just me not thinking it through.

  Why the motor gets hot:

  A turning motor does two things that keep it cool. It generates back-EMF,
  which opposes the supply and naturally limits the current. And it converts
  electrical energy into mechanical work — energy that leaves as motion.

  A stalled motor does neither. No back-EMF, so current is limited only by
  resistance and the controller's ceiling. No work out, so essentially every
  watt becomes I²R heat in the windings. And because the rotor isn't turning,
  the current doesn't rotate between phases — the same one or two windings carry
  it continuously, so the heat concentrates in one spot rather than spreading
  around the stator.

  We were putting 3.45 A through it at the 6 Nm limit, for seconds at a time,
  over and over.

  And the two questions have the same root. With a wrong flux offset, current is
  injected at the wrong electrical angle: it produces very little torque but
  exactly the same heating. That's the worst case — full current, no motion, all
  of it heat. The reason it doesn't move and the reason it cooks are the same
  fault.

  When you're ready — motor cool, arm wherever it can turn outward — the test
  is: flip phase_order on ID 4, then run one calibration and watch which way it
  goes. One run, one direction, and I'll stop there so you can look before
  anything else happens.
