● ID 4 works.

  ┌─────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     │                                         │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ sweeps              │ 4053 and 4056 counts — 99% both times   │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ flux                │ 67.475 and 67.550 — repeatable to 0.075 │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ commanded +0.15 rad │ moved +0.1412 (+8.1°) on 0.22 A         │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ commanded −0.15 rad │ moved −0.1380 (−7.9°)                   │
  └─────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────┘

  It follows commands in both directions, and it does it on 0.22 A — less
  current than its healthy twin needs. Yesterday it sat motionless at 6
  newton-metres.

  The fix was your idea: less calibration current, not more. One amp, where the
  working joints use three to five. The calibration pulls the rotor to a known
  angle and then rotates the field; too much current and it slams into alignment
  and locks there instead of following. Every attempt to force it — mine at 8 A
  especially — was making it worse, and the heat you felt was the direct
  result.

  And the flux repeatability settles the last open question. Earlier I told you
  the scattered values meant a slipping magnet or a shifting rotor. They didn't.
  They were all measured from sweeps that never completed. Given a sweep that
  actually turns, this motor measures itself to within 0.075 — a perfectly
  healthy, stable result.

  So: not a dead phase, not a loose magnet, not a jammed gearbox, not a fried
  ESC, not a mechanical seizure. Every one of those was me reading a failed
  measurement as data. It was a calibration current three to eight times higher
  than this particular motor could tolerate.

  That's nine of ten joints working. The only one left is the left wrist, can0/9
  — and it shows the same one-way symptom, its calibration current currently
  reads 5 A, and it has never had a complete sweep either.

  Want me to try 1 A on it?

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● ID 4 is fixed — that's nine of your ten joints. It follows commands both ways
  now, on 0.22 A, having been immovable at 6 Nm yesterday. The cause was
  MOTOR_MAX_CALIBRATION_CURRENT set far too high: it needed 1.0 A where every
  other joint uses 3–5.

  That was your call, not mine. I spent two days pushing more current at it and
  had it at 8 A for hours — which, since heating goes as current squared, is
  also why it got hot enough for you to feel.

  Everything I diagnosed before that was a failed measurement I read as data: a
  dead phase (measured at a stationary rotor), a loose magnet (flux scatter from
  sweeps that never completed), a seized gearbox (joint parked against a stop),
  a dead encoder (five reads of a joint that wasn't moving), and two "full
  sweeps" that were a wrap-around artifact. The joint was healthy the whole
  time.

  L wrist is a genuinely different problem. At every current from 0.5 to 1.25 A
  it starts near raw 2275 and stops at 2448 counts — 60% of a revolution, about 
  14° — with a flux that repeats at ~28.5. A calibration needs 24°. So that
  consistent flux is a consistent measurement of the wrong thing, which is why
  it manages 4% of a commanded move while its twin does 99%.

  Its travel is clearly shorter than the right wrist's. Two possibilities: it's
  parked against a stop and returns there each time, or something is genuinely
  restricting it.

  The next step needs your hands for about ten seconds: move the left wrist by
  hand to the far end of its travel and tell me. I'll calibrate from there at 1
  A, and if it gets a full sweep, that joint follows too. If it still stops at
  2448, its range really is 14° and that's an assembly question — cable routing
  or a stop — rather than anything I can fix from here.

  run_teleop was still starting when I checked; give it a few seconds.

  everything from flash, and re-initialises the encoder. If the wrist's problem
  is a state the board has got itself into, this clears it. If it comes back
  behaving identically, that's useful too — it points firmly at the physical
  connection rather than anything in software.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● There's a difference nobody has looked at.

  ┌──────────────────┬─────────────┬───────────────────┐
  │                  │ flux scalar │ flux offset TABLE │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ L wrist (broken) │ 70.13       │ 1096263701        │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ R wrist (works)  │ 58.61       │ 0                 │
  └──────────────────┴─────────────┴───────────────────┘

  The working wrist has an empty table. The broken one has a large non-zero
  value sitting in ENCODER_FLUX_OFFSET_TABLE.

  That parameter exists for position-dependent commutation correction. If the
  firmware is applying a table entry on top of the scalar offset, the effective
  commutation angle would be wrong regardless of what I set the scalar to —
  which would explain every single thing this joint has done: full torque
  delivered into the wrong electrical angle, no motion, and a scalar offset that
  makes no difference no matter what value I write.

  It also explains why the rare successes were unrepeatable, and why restoring
  the good scalar by hand achieved nothing.

  Let me check the rest of the fleet before touching it, because if every
  working joint reads 0 and only this one doesn't, that's the anomaly we've been
  hunting for two days:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● That's the anomaly. Nine joints read 0. The broken one reads 1096263701 — and
  nothing else distinguishes it.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● It decodes to 13.4785 — a second flux offset, sitting in a table that every
  working joint has at zero. If the firmware applies it on top of the scalar,
  the commutation angle is wrong no matter what I write to the scalar. That
  would explain everything: full torque into the wrong electrical angle, and a
  scalar that never mattered.

  Setting it to match the nine that work:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Zeroing it made no difference — 0 of 6. So that table value is a genuine
  anomaly (nine joints at zero, this one not) but it isn't the cause.

  I'm going to stop guessing and name the one experiment I've never actually
  run:

  Every time the first sweep after a fresh state has completed — 3931, 3957,
  4029 counts on three separate occasions — I immediately ran a second sweep to 
  check repeatability, that one failed, and it overwrote the good flux. I have
  never once tested the joint straight after a successful calibration. Restoring
  the number by hand afterwards isn't the same thing, since the calibration
  evidently sets state beyond that one scalar.

  anyone about the firmware.

  calibration completes, test it before you verify it.

● ID 4's fix held through the power cycle — phase +1, 1.0 A, flux 67.55,
  unchanged. That one's solid.

  The only joint that changed is the wrist, and only where I changed it myself.

  On the wrist, the experiment didn't get its chance: the single calibration
  after this boot came back at 72%, not the full sweep the pattern predicted. So
  I still haven't managed to test it straight after a complete calibration. One
  command did move 4.6° at 67% — but in the wrong direction, and the five after
  it gave nothing.

  I'm calling it on remote debugging for this joint. Across two days I've tried
  both phase orders, calibration currents from 0.5 to 8 A, flux offsets from six
  sources including hand-written ones, multiple positions, the flux offset
  table, two power cycles, cold starts, step and ramp commands, and torque from
  1 to 5 Nm. It has followed a command three times, never repeatably, and every
  measurement of its electronics comes back healthy.

  That pattern — works occasionally, nothing predicts when, everything measures
  fine — is what a marginal connection looks like. The next useful step is 
  physical: reseat the motor and encoder connectors on can0/9 and check the 
  loom. This robot has had exactly that fault twice before, on ID 5 and on ID 9
  itself.

  Where you finished: nine of ten joints working, ID 4 rescued today by your
  instinct to reduce the calibration current, and a set of tools that now refuse
  to report the kind of false measurements that had me chasing a dead phase, a
  loose magnet and a seized gearbox on a joint that was healthy the whole time.

● The firmware has a 128-entry flux offset lookup table, populated during
  calibration. Let me read the calibration routine itself:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Now I can see exactly how it works — and I made a mistake earlier.

  The LUT is 128 entries, and calibration writes all of them:
  flux_offset_table[lut_index] = moving_avg - flux_offset for every index. I 
  only zeroed entry 0. The other 127 could still hold garbage from all those
  failed sweeps.
